Relational Algebra A Comprehensive Course: Relational Databases & NoSQL — Chapter4

Chapter4Relational Algebra

Chapter Overview

Understanding relational algebra is essential for grasping how query optimizers execute, transform, and evaluate database queries under the hood. The chapter covers the fundamental definitions, five primary vocabulary terms, and the core operations:

  • Unary Operations: Selection (σ), Projection (π), and Rename (ρ).

  • Set-Theoretic Operations: Union (∪), Intersection (∩), Set Difference (−), and Cartesian Product (×).

  • Joins & Advanced Concepts: How foreign keys and conditions transform raw Cartesian products into meaningful joins across normalized relations.

🎯

Learning Outcomes

At the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

  1. Explain the difference between declarative SQL statements and procedural relational algebra expressions.

  2. Define and correctly apply core terms: Relation, Tuple, Attribute, Degree (number of columns), and Cardinality (number of rows).

  3. Filter relation rows using the Selection (σ) operator with logical and comparison conditions.

  4. Filter relation columns using the Projection (π) operator and understand its automatic duplicate elimination property.

  5. Rename relations and attributes using the Rename (ρ) operator to resolve column name conflicts during complex operations.

  6. Evaluate Union (∪), Intersection (∩), and Set Difference (−) while enforcing union-compatibility rules (matching degrees and compatible attribute domains).

  7. Compute the Cartesian Product (×) of two relations and calculate the resulting degree and cardinality.

  8. Explain why unconditioned Cartesian products create invalid data pairs and how primary/foreign key relationships filter these into meaningful join operations.

  9. Convert standard SQL queries (SELECT, WHERE, JOIN, UNION, EXCEPT, INTERSECT) into equivalent relational algebra expressions.

1What Is Relational Algebra?

SQL is a declarative language: you say what you want; the DBMS decides how to compute it. The “how” is determined by translating your SQL into relational algebra — a procedural formal language where every operation is an explicit step. Understanding relational algebra means understanding how the query optimizer thinks, why certain queries are fast, and what is happening beneath every JOIN you write.

📖Relational Algebra

Relational algebra is a procedural formal query language for relations. Each operation takes one or two relations as input and returns a new relation as output.

Closure property

The result of every relational algebra operation is itself a relation. This means operations can be freely composed — the output of one becomes the input of the next.

Expression=Opk​(…Op2​(Op1​(R1​,R2​))…)

There are 6 fundamental operations (from which all others can be derived) and several convenient additional (derived) operations.

GroupSub-Category / TypeOperations
Fundamental (Basic)UnarySelection, Projection, Rename
Set-TheoreticUnion, Set Difference, Cartesian Product
Derived (Additional)Set-TheoreticSet Intersection
JoinsNatural Join, Theta Join, Outer Joins, Semijoins
DivisionDivision
ExtendedAggregationsAggregate Functions (Group By)

2Fundamental Concepts in Relational Algebra

Before you can work with relational algebra operators, you need to know the vocabulary. The good news is that there are only five terms to remember.

  • Relation is just a table. It has rows and columns, and it represents a set of data about a specific entity - like employees or departments. In relational algebra, every operation takes one or more relations as input and produces a relation as output.

  • Tuple is a single row in that table. If your Employees table has 10 rows, it has 10 tuples. Each tuple represents one record - one employee, in this case.

  • Attribute is a column. The Employees table might have attributes like employee_id, name, and department_id. Attributes define what kind of data each tuple holds.

  • Degree is the number of attributes in a relation. If Employees has five columns, its degree is 5. This matters when you're combining relations - some operations require matching degrees.

  • Cardinality is the number of tuples in a relation. If your Employees table has 200 rows, its cardinality is 200. Cardinality changes as you add or remove rows; degree changes only when you add or remove columns.

3Fundamental Operations: Unary Operations (Operate on a single relation)

3.1The SELECT Operation — σ (Filter Rows)

The SELECT operation is used to select a subset of the tuples from a relation that satisfy a selection condition. One can consider the SELECT operation to be a filter that keeps only those tuples that satisfy a qualifying condition. The SELECT operation can also be visualized as a horizontal partition of the relation into two sets of tuples-those tuples that satisfy the condition and are selected, and those tuples that do not satisfy the condition and are discarded.

σcondition​(R)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
σ condition (R)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM R WHERE condition
PropertyValue
InputRelation R
Output columnsSame as R (degree unchanged)
Output rowsAll tuples where condition is true (cardinality ≤ ∣R∣)
Condition operators=,≠,<,>,≤,≥,combined with ∧ (AND), ∨ (OR), ¬ (NOT)

📝Selection by Salary Threshold

List all employees whose salary is greater than 65,000.

✅ Show Solution
σSalary>65000​(Employee)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
σ Salary > 65000 (Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE Salary > 65000

List all employees who do not work in department 2.

✅ Show Solution
σ¬(Dno=2)​Employee
Relational algebra (RELAX)
σ ¬ Dno = 2 (Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE NOT Dno = 2

3.2The PROJECT Operation— π (Filter Columns)

While Selection operation works on rows, similarly projection operation of relational algebra works on columns. It basically allows us to pick specific columns from a given relational table based on the given condition and ignoring all the other remaining columns.

Note

By Default, projection operation removes duplicate values.

πA1​,A2​,…,Ak​​(R)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
π A_1, A_2, A_k (R)
SQL equivalent
SELECT DISTINCT A_1, A_2, A_k FROM R

📝Simple Projection

List the first name, last name, and salary of every employee.

✅ Show Solution
πFname,Lname,Salary​(Employee)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
π Fname, Lname, Salary (Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT DISTINCT Fname, Lname, Salary FROM Employee

List the first name and salary of all female employees earning above 60,000.

✅ Show Solution
πFname,Salary​(σGender=′F′∧Salary>60000​(Employee))
Relational algebra (RELAX)
π Fname, Salary (σ Gender = 'F' ∧ Salary > 60000 (Employee))
SQL equivalent
SELECT DISTINCT Fname, Salary FROM (SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE Gender = 'F' AND Salary > 60000) AS t1

π FIRST_NAME (ρ FIRST_NAME←Fname (Employee))

3.3The Rename Operation— ρ (Renames a relation or its attribute names)

Rename operator basically allows you to give a temporary name to a specific relational table or to its columns. It is very useful when we want to avoid ambiguity, especially in complex Queries. Rename is a unary operation used for renaming attributes of a relation.

Rename Relation
ρ S​(R)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
ρ S (R)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM R AS S
Rename Attributes
ρ att1_new←att1,att2_new←att2​(R)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
ρ att1_new←att1, att2_new←att2 (R)
Rename Relation and Attributes
ρ R_new​(ρ att1_new←att1​(πatt1,att2​(R)))
Relational algebra (RELAX)
ρ R_new (ρ att1_new←att1 (π att1, att2 (R)))

📝

Create a named subexpression Supervisor containing SSN, first name, and last name of all employees, using shorter column names.

✅ Show Solution
ρ Supervisor​(π Ssn,Fname,Lname​(Employee))
Relational algebra (RELAX)
ρ Supervisor (π Ssn, Fname, Lname (Employee))
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT DISTINCT Ssn, Fname, Lname FROM Employee) AS Supervisor

Logic

Join

4Set-Theoretic Operations (Operate on two relations)

Set-theoretic operations in relational algebra are binary operators that combine or compare tuples from two relations, treating each relation as a formal set of tuples.

let’s R(A1​,A2​,...,An​)and S(B1​,B2​,...,Bn​)are relations. The Set Compatibility Rules - ∪,∩, and − all require union-compatible operands:

  1. Degree(R) = Degree(S): have the same number of attributes.

  2. Domain(Ai) = Domain(Bi) , for 1<= i <=n: Corresponding attributes must have compatible domains(same or convertible types).

4.1Union — ∪ (Combine Two Relations)

📖Union (R ∪ S)

Returns all tuples that belong to relation R, relation S, or both. The result takes the attribute names from the left operand(R)

R∪S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ∪ S)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM R
UNION
SELECT * FROM S

📝— Union

Find all SSNs that appear in either the Employee table or the Works_On table.

✅ Show Solution
πSsn​(Employee) ∪ πEssn​(Works_On)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(π Ssn (Employee) ∪ π Essn (Works_On))
SQL equivalent
SELECT DISTINCT Ssn FROM Employee
UNION
SELECT DISTINCT Essn FROM Works_On

📝— Union

Retrieve the Social Security Numbers (Ssn) for all employees who work in Department 3 or directly supervise an employee in Department 3

✅ Show Solution

To solve this using Relational Algebra, we break the problem into two parts and combine them using the Union operator:

  1. Find employees working in Department 3.

  2. Find supervisors of employees working in Department 3

  3. Combine results using Union.

R1​=πSsn​(σDno=3​(Employee))R2​=πSuper_ssn​(σDno=3​(Employee))Result=R1​∪R2​
Relational algebra (RELAX)
R_1 = π Ssn (σ Dno = 3 (Employee))
R_2 = π Super_ssn (σ Dno = 3 (Employee))
(R_1 ∪ R_2)
SQL equivalent
WITH R_1 AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT Ssn FROM (SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE Dno = 3) AS t1
),
R_2 AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT Super_ssn FROM (SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE Dno = 3) AS t2
),
Result AS (
  SELECT * FROM R_1
UNION
SELECT * FROM R_2
)
SELECT * FROM Result

4.2Intersection — ∩

📖Intersection (R ∩ S)

Returns only the tuples that are present in both relation R and relation S.

R∩S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ∩ S)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM R
INTERSECT
SELECT * FROM S

Note

Intersection is a derived operation — it can always be rewritten using set difference.R∩S=R−(R−S)

📝Intersection

Retrieve the Male Employees that work in department 1

✅ Show Solution

Steps:

  • Retrieve all employees in department 1 as R1.

  • Retrieve all Male employees in Employee as R2.

  • Result = R1 INTERSECTION R2.

R1​=σDno=1​(Employee)R2​=σGender=′M′​(Employee)Result=R1​∩R2​
Relational algebra (RELAX)
R_1 = σ Dno = 1 (Employee)
R_2 = σ Gender = 'M' (Employee)
(R_1 ∩ R_2)
SQL equivalent
WITH R_1 AS (
  SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE Dno = 1
),
R_2 AS (
  SELECT * FROM Employee WHERE Gender = 'M'
),
Result AS (
  SELECT * FROM R_1
INTERSECT
SELECT * FROM R_2
)
SELECT * FROM Result

4.3Set Difference — − (In One But Not the Other)

📖Set Difference (R - S)

Returns all tuples that are present in relation R but not present in relation S. Requires union compatibility.

R−S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R - S)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM R
EXCEPT
SELECT * FROM S

5Cartesian Product — × (All Combinations)

Unary relational algebra operations like Select and Project operate on a single relation at a time. However, in real-world relational databases, data is organized according to normalization principles to eliminate redundancy, avoid data anomalies, and maintain data integrity. As a result, related information is split across multiple tables (for example, storing employee details in an Employee relation and department details in a Department relation).

To answer queries that require information spanning multiple tables, we need a mechanism to correlate tuples across relations:

  • Combining Schemas: The Cartesian Product ($R \times S$) serves as the fundamental relational algebra operation that pairs every tuple in R with every tuple in S. This generates a new relation containing the combined attributes of both relations (Degree: degree(R) + degree(S)).

  • The Foundation for Joins: While a raw Cartesian Product generates all possible combinations—including many non-matching or irrelevant pairs—it forms the mathematical foundation for Join operations. When followed by a Selection on a matching key condition (such as R.Dno = S.Dnumber), it narrows down the combined dataset to extract meaningful, related records across normalized tables.

Summary

Without the Cartesian Product (and the join operations built upon it), a relational system could only query individual, isolated tables, defeating the core purpose of a normalized relational database design.

R×S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ⨯ S)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM R) AS t1 CROSS JOIN (SELECT * FROM S) AS t2
Table 23:Relation R
r1r2

34
56

s1

Table 25:R X S
r1r2s1

PropertyValue

Important

The raw Cartesian product generates 30 × 8 = 240 rows but most are meaningless combinations. It is almost always followed by a σσ to keep only matching rows — that combination is precisely a join.

📝Column Name Conflict

Try to pair every employee with every other employee using a Cartesian product.

✅ Show Solution
Employee×Employee
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(Employee ⨯ Employee)
Relational Algebra Calculator
RA ops
Assign
Logic
Compare
Set
Join
Misc


Why Employee × Employee Fails (Column Name Conflict)
When you write Employee × Employee, both copies of the table bring the same 10 column names (Fname, Lname, Ssn, …).
A relation must have unique column names — there is no way to tell Fname (left copy) from Fname (right copy).
The correct approach: use ρ to rename one (or both) copies before forming the product:


ρE1​(Employee)×ρE2​(Employee)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(ρ E1 (Employee) ⨯ ρ E2 (Employee))
Relational Algebra Calculator

The general rule
every column in a relation must be uniquely identifiable. Whenever a Cartesian product would produce duplicate column names, use ρ to rename one operand before the product.



5.1Joining Multiple Tables using Foreign Keys



When information is spread across multiple tables, we must combine (merge) them to retrieve the data we need.
  • Why Unconditioned Merges Fail: Performing a Cartesian product (merging tables without a condition) pairs every row of the first table with every row of the second table, creating meaningless and illogical combinations. 
  • Using Foreign Keys as Join Conditions: To ensure we extract accurate and logical data, we must take advantage of foreign key relationships. Using the foreign key as a join condition (PK = FK) filters out invalid pairs, guaranteeing that only truly related records are merged.



📝
Retrieve all Female dependent names for each employee.
✅ Show Solution
To retrieve the names of all dependents associated with female employees, you must combine the Employee relation and the Dependent relation using their foreign key relationship.
  1. Perform the Cartesian Product: All Pairs←Employee×Dependent
  2. Apply the Selection (Filter for matching Foreign Key AND Female Gender):FemaleDependents←σSsn=Essn∧Gender=’F’​(AllPairs)
  3. Project the Desired Attribute:Result←πDependent_name​(FemaleDependents)


Emp=πSsn,Fname,Lname​(Employee)AllPairs←Emp×DependentFemaleDependents←σSsn=Essn∧Gender=’F’​(AllPairs)Result←πDependent_name,Fname,Lname​(FemaleDependents)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
Emp = π Ssn, Fname, Lname (Employee)
AllPairs = (Emp ⨯ Dependent)
FemaleDependents = σ Ssn = Essn ∧Gender = 'F' (AllPairs)
π Dependent_name, Fname, Lname (FemaleDependents)

6Joins



📖Join Operation
A join operation in relational algebra combines tuples (rows) from two separate relations (tables) based on a specific condition, acting fundamentally as a Cartesian product followed by a selection.



6.1Theta Join (Cartesian Product + Filter)



📖A theta-join (⋈θ)
A theta-join (⋈θ) is a relational algebra operation that combines two relations based on any general comparison or conditional operator, rather than strictly equality. It is formally defined as performing a Cartesian product first and then immediately applying a selection condition (σ) to filter out unrelated combinations.



R⋈θ​S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ⨝ [standard condition] S)



where: 
  • General Comparison Operators: Unlike an equi-join (which strictly uses equality $=$), a theta-join allows any standard condition operator (θ∈{=,=,<,≤,>,≥}).
  • Formal Algebraic Equivalence: Formally, a theta-join between two relations R and S is defined as performing a Cartesian Product first and then applying a Selection (σ) condition:
    R⋈θ​S≡σθ​(R×S)
    Where θ represents the join condition (e.g., R.A>S.B or R.Ssn=S.Essn). 



Tip
A Theta Join (⋈θ​) is simply a general form of an inner join.
  • Mechanism: It combines two relations by pairing rows that satisfy a specified condition θ (e.g., =,=,<,>,≤,≥).
  • Inner Join Behavior: Only tuples that evaluate to true under condition θ are included in the result set; unmatched tuples are discarded.
  • Equivalence: A Theta Join is formally defined as a Cartesian product followed by a Selection:
    Relation1​⋈θ​Relation2​≡σθ​(Relation1​×Relation2​)
  • An equijoin is simply a specific type of theta join where the condition relies strictly on the equality operator (=). While all equijoins are theta joins, not all theta joins are equijoins (as theta joins can use any comparison operator, such as >, <, or =).



📝
List each employee with their project number and hours, joining Employee and Works_On on Ssn = Essn.
✅ Show Solution
Employee⋈Ssn=Essn​Works_On
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(Employee ⨝(Ssn = Essn) Works_On)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM Employee) AS t1 JOIN (SELECT * FROM Works_On) AS t2 ON Ssn = Essn

            '019', 'Majd Zahr', 'M', '2020-03-15', 'Son'
            '022', 'Tala Jabari', 'F', '2015-11-08', 'Daughter'
            }


Why it is called an Equijoin here?
Because the specific condition chosen for θ is an equality comparison (Ssn=Essn), this theta join qualifies specifically as an equijoin.



6.2Natural Join —  ⋈



📖Natural Join
a Natural Join (⋈) is a derived relational algebra operation that automatically performs a join based on all attributes that share the same name in both relations.



Key Properties: 
  • Implicit Equality Condition: It matches rows where all shared attribute names have equal values (an implicit equijoin).
  • Duplicate Column Elimination: Unlike a Cartesian product or theta join, duplicate common attributes are automatically removed—appearing only once in the resulting relation's schema.



Result Schema Rule
If relation R has attributes (A,B,C) and relation S has attributes (B,C,D), then the natural join R⋈S results in a relation with attributes (A,B,C,D).



R⋈S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ⨝ S)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM R) AS t1 NATURAL JOIN (SELECT * FROM S) AS t2



Natural Join is Name-Driven
Natural join joins on every column that shares the same name in both tables.
  • If PK and FK have different names → no common columns → result is a Cartesian product (all combinations, probably wrong)
  • If you accidentally share too many column names → the join condition becomes overly restrictive
  • Always verify which columns will be matched before using natural join



📝Natural Join: When It Works Directly
List each department with all its office locations.
✅ Show Solution
  • The Department table has Dnumber as its primary key.
  • The Dept_Locations table has Dnumber as a foreign key.
  • Both columns share the exact same name → natural join will automatically join on Dnumber.


Department⋈Dept_Locations
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(Department ⨝ Dept_Locations)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM Department) AS t1 NATURAL JOIN (SELECT * FROM Dept_Locations) AS t2

📝Natural Join: When Column Names Differ (Danger!)
Try to list each employee with the name of their department using natural join.
✅ Show Solution
Employee.Dno is the FK referencing Department.Dnumber — but the column names are different.
Since there are no columns with identical names between Employee and Department, a natural join produces a Cartesian product — every employee paired with every department, regardless of which department they belong to.


Employee⋈Department
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(Employee ⨝ Department)

6.3Outer Joins



Standard (inner) joins discard unmatched rows. Outer joins preserve them by filling missing values with NULL.



OperatorSymbolKeeps unmatched from
Left outer joinR⟕SR (left operand)
Right outer joinR⟖SS (right operand)
Full outer join R ⟗ SBoth  R R and  S S



6.3.1Left Outer Join(R ⟕ S)



📖Left Outer Join
A Left Outer Join is an extended relational algebra operation that returns all matching tuples from both relations, plus all unmatched tuples from the left relation.



R ⟕ S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ⟕ S)



Key Properties:
  • Preserves Left Relation Tuples: Guarantees that every tuple from the left relation appears in the result at least once.
  • NULL Padding for Unmatched Rows: If a tuple in R has no matching tuple in S based on the join condition, it is included in the output schema with NULL values for all attributes originating from S.
  • Schema / Output Columns: The output schema contains all attributes from both R and S.



📝
Retrieve all employees along with their corresponding department information, even if some employees do not belong to any department.
✅ Show Solution
Employee ⟕ Dno=Dnumber​Department
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(Employee ⟕(Dno = Dnumber) Department)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM Employee) AS t1 LEFT JOIN (SELECT * FROM Department) AS t2 ON Dno = Dnumber

6.3.2Right Outer Join(R ⟖ S)



📖Right Outer Join
A Right Outer Join is an extended relational algebra operation that returns all matching tuples from both relations, plus all unmatched tuples from the right relation.



R ⟖ S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ⟖ S)



Key Properties:
  • Preserves Right Relation Tuples: Guarantees that every tuple from the right relation appears in the result at least once.
  • NULL Padding for Unmatched Rows: If a tuple in S has no matching tuple in R based on the join condition, it is included in the output schema with NULL values for all attributes originating from R.
  • Schema / Output Columns: The output schema contains all attributes from both R and S.



📝
Retrieve all departments along with their corresponding employee information, even if some departments do not have any employees assigned to them.
✅ Show Solution
Department ⟖Dno=Dnumber​Employee
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(Department ⟖(Dno = Dnumber) Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM Department) AS t1 RIGHT JOIN (SELECT * FROM Employee) AS t2 ON Dno = Dnumber

6.3.3Full Outer Join(R ⟗ S)



📖Full Outer Join
A Full Outer Join is an extended relational algebra operation that combines the results of both a Left Outer Join and a Right Outer Join. It returns all matching tuples from both relations, along with all unmatched tuples from both the left and right relations.



R ⟗ S
Relational algebra (RELAX)
(R ⟗ S)



Key Properties:
  • Preserves All Tuples: Guarantees that every tuple from both relations (R and S) appears in the result at least once
  • Unmatched tuples from the left relation (R) are padded with NULL values for all attributes of the right relation (S).
  • Unmatched tuples from the right relation (S) are padded with NULL values for all attributes of the left relation (R)
  • Schema / Output Columns: The output schema contains all attributes from both relations R and S.



📝
List all employees, all project assignments, and all projects—including employees who are not assigned to any project, project assignment records without a matching employee, and projects that have zero employees assigned to them.
✅ Show Solution
Employee ⟗Ssn=Essn​Works_On ⟗Pno=Pnumber​Project
Relational algebra (RELAX)
((Employee ⟗(Ssn = Essn) Works_On) ⟗(Pno = Pnumber) Project)
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM Employee) AS t1 FULL OUTER JOIN (SELECT * FROM Works_On) AS t2 ON Ssn = Essn) AS t3 FULL OUTER JOIN (SELECT * FROM Project) AS t4 ON Pno = Pnumber

7Extended Relational Algebra — Aggregation (γ)



Standard relational algebra operates on sets of tuples with no grouping or counting. Real queries often need summaries: “count employees per department”, “average salary by gender”, “maximum project hours”. The extended relational algebra adds the aggregate operator γ (gamma).



γ G​;F(A)→N (R)
Relational algebra (RELAX)



where:
  • γ (Gamma): Represents the extended relational algebra aggregation operator.
  • G is grouping attribute (equivalent to SQL GROUP BY).
  • F(A): The aggregate function F applied to attribute A. 
  • N: alias/name to the computed result.
  • R: The input relation/table or subquery.



Table 26:Aggregate functions
FunctionMeaning
COUNT()Count rows in each group
SUM(col)Sum of values
AVG(col)Average of values
MIN(col)Minimum value
MAX(col)Maximum value



📝Count Employees per Department
How many employees work in each department?
✅ Show Solution
γDno;COUNT()→NumEmp(Employee)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
γ Dno;COUNT(*)→NumEmp (Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT Dno, COUNT(*) AS NumEmp FROM Employee GROUP BY Dno

📝Average Salary per Department
What is the average salary in each department?
✅ Show Solution
γDno;AVG(Salary)→AvgSalary(Employee)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
γ Dno;AVG(Salary)→AvgSalary (Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT Dno, AVG(Salary) AS AvgSalary FROM Employee GROUP BY Dno

📝Multiple Aggregates: Max and Min Salary per Department
In each department, what is the highest and lowest salary?
✅ Show Solution
γDno;MAX(Salary)→MaxSal,MIN(Salary)→MinSal(Employee)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
γ Dno;MAX(Salary)→MaxSal,MIN(Salary)→MinSal (Employee)
SQL equivalent
SELECT Dno, MAX(Salary) AS MaxSal, MIN(Salary) AS MinSal FROM Employee GROUP BY Dno

📝Aggregation with Filter: Departments Averaging Over $33,000
List department numbers where the average salary exceeds $33,000.
✅ Show Solution
σAvgSalary>33000​(γDno;AVG(Salary)→AvgSalary(Employee))
Relational algebra (RELAX)
σ AvgSalary > 33000 (γ Dno;AVG(Salary)→AvgSalary (Employee))
SQL equivalent
SELECT * FROM (SELECT Dno, AVG(Salary) AS AvgSalary FROM Employee GROUP BY Dno) AS t1 WHERE AvgSalary > 33000

📝Total Hours per Employee Across All Projects
For each employee, calculate the total hours worked across all their project assignments.
✅ Show Solution
γEssn;SUM(Hours)→TotalHours(WorksO​n)
Relational algebra (RELAX)
γ Essn;SUM(Hours)→TotalHours (Works_On)
SQL equivalent
SELECT Essn, SUM(Hours) AS TotalHours FROM Works_On GROUP BY Essn

8Exercises



8.1University Database



8.1.1University Database Schema Overview



The University Database schema is designed to model core academic and administrative operations within a higher education institution. It manages information across six key entities: students, faculty, departments, courses, course enrollments, and teaching assignments.



8.1.1.1Entity Descriptions & Key Relationships



  • Department
    • Attributes: Did (Primary Key), Dname, Chair_id (Foreign Key → Faculty.Fid), Building. 
    • Description: Represents academic departments within the university. Each department operates in a specific building and is chaired by a faculty member. 
  • Faculty
    • Attributes: Fid (Primary Key), Fname, Lname, Rank, Salary, Dept_id (Foreign Key → Department.Did). 
    • Description: Represents academic staff members. Each faculty member belongs to a specific department and holds an academic rank (e.g., Professor, AssocProf, AssistProf). 
  • Student
    • Attributes: Sid (Primary Key), Fname, Lname, Email, Major, GPA, Year, Advisor_id (Foreign Key → Faculty.Fid). 
    • Description: Stores student demographic and academic performance data. Each student is assigned a faculty member as their academic advisor. 
  • Course
    • Attributes: Cid (Primary Key), Cname, Credits, Dept_id (Foreign Key → Department.Did). 
    • Description: Defines subjects offered by the university. Every course belongs to an offering department and carries a specified number of academic credit units. 
  • Enrollment (Junction Table for Student–Course M:N Relationship)
    • Attributes: Sid (Foreign Key → Student.Sid), Cid (Foreign Key → Course.Cid), Semester, Grade. 
    • Composite Primary Key: (Sid, Cid, Semester)
    • Description: Tracks student course registrations and academic performance (letter grades) across different academic terms. 
  • Teaches (Junction Table for Faculty–Course M:N Relationship)
    • Attributes: Fid (Foreign Key → Faculty.Fid), Cid (Foreign Key → Course.Cid), Semester, Room. 
    • Composite Primary Key: (Fid, Cid, Semester)
    • Description: Maps teaching responsibilities, indicating which faculty member teaches a specific course section, semester, and assigned classroom location. 



8.1.1.2Schema Reference



  • Student (Sid,Fname,Lname,Email,Major,GPA,Year,Advisor_id)
  • Faculty (Fid,Fname,Lname,Rank,Salary,Dept_id)
  • Department (Did,Dname,Chair_id,Building)
  • Course (Cid,Cname,Credits,Dept_id)
  • Enrollment (Sid,Cid,Semester,Grade)
  • Teaches (Fid,Cid,Semester,Room)



Diagram 54:University Database
